\section{Cryptography and PKI}
\begin{qcard}{\color{white}\textbf{Question 1} \hfill \small Multiple Choice \textbar{} Topic: Cryptography and PKI}{multicol}
Which are asymmetric algorithms? (Choose all that apply)

\begin{qoptions}
\item RSA
\item ECC
\item DSA
\item AES
\end{qoptions}
\begin{explainbox}{A,B,C}
\textbf{Concept:} RSA, ECC, and DSA are asymmetric; AES is symmetric.
\end{explainbox}
\end{qcard}

\begin{qcard}{\color{white}\textbf{Question 2} \hfill \small Multiple Choice \textbar{} Topic: Cryptography and PKI}{multicol}
Which are block cipher modes? (Choose all that apply)

\begin{qoptions}
\item ECB
\item CBC
\item CTR
\item GCM
\end{qoptions}
\begin{explainbox}{A,B,C,D}
\textbf{Concept:} ECB, CBC, CTR, and GCM are all AES block cipher modes.
\end{explainbox}
\end{qcard}

\begin{qcard}{\color{white}\textbf{Question 3} \hfill \small Multiple Choice \textbar{} Topic: Cryptography and PKI}{multicol}
Which are secure hash algorithms? (Choose all that apply)

\begin{qoptions}
\item SHA-256
\item SHA-3
\item BLAKE2
\item MD5
\end{qoptions}
\begin{explainbox}{A,B,C}
\textbf{Concept:} SHA-256, SHA-3, and BLAKE2 are considered secure; MD5 is broken.
\end{explainbox}
\end{qcard}

\begin{qcard}{\color{white}\textbf{Question 4} \hfill \small Multiple Choice \textbar{} Topic: Cryptography and PKI}{multicol}
Which are PKI trust models? (Choose all that apply)

\begin{qoptions}
\item Single CA
\item Hierarchical CA
\item Cross-certification
\item Bridge CA
\end{qoptions}
\begin{explainbox}{A,B,C,D}
\textbf{Concept:} PKI can use single, hierarchical, cross-certified, or bridge CA models.
\end{explainbox}
\end{qcard}

\begin{qcard}{\color{white}\textbf{Question 5} \hfill \small Multiple Choice \textbar{} Topic: Cryptography and PKI}{multicol}
Which fields are typically found in an X.509 certificate? (Choose all that apply)

\begin{qoptions}
\item Subject
\item Issuer
\item Public key
\item Validity period
\end{qoptions}
\begin{explainbox}{A,B,C,D}
\textbf{Concept:} X.509 certificates contain subject, issuer, public key, validity, serial number, and signature.
\end{explainbox}
\end{qcard}

\begin{qcard}{\color{white}\textbf{Question 6} \hfill \small Multiple Choice \textbar{} Topic: Cryptography and PKI}{multicol}
Which are symmetric algorithms? (Choose all that apply)

\begin{qoptions}
\item AES
\item DES
\item 3DES
\item RSA
\end{qoptions}
\begin{explainbox}{A,B,C}
\textbf{Concept:} AES, DES, and 3DES are symmetric; RSA is asymmetric.
\end{explainbox}
\end{qcard}

\begin{qcard}{\color{white}\textbf{Question 7} \hfill \small Multiple Choice \textbar{} Topic: Cryptography and PKI}{multicol}
Which are hash algorithms? (Choose all that apply)

\begin{qoptions}
\item MD5
\item SHA-256
\item SHA-1
\item SHA-3
\end{qoptions}
\begin{explainbox}{A,B,C,D}
\textbf{Concept:} These are all hash algorithms, though MD5 and SHA-1 are deprecated for security use.
\end{explainbox}
\end{qcard}

\begin{qcard}{\color{white}\textbf{Question 8} \hfill \small Multiple Choice \textbar{} Topic: Cryptography and PKI}{multicol}
Which are PKI components? (Choose all that apply)

\begin{qoptions}
\item CA
\item RA
\item Repository
\item CRL/OCSP
\end{qoptions}
\begin{explainbox}{A,B,C,D}
\textbf{Concept:} PKI includes certificate authorities, registration authorities, repositories, and revocation systems.
\end{explainbox}
\end{qcard}

\part{True / False Questions}
\section{Network Security Concepts}
\begin{qcard}{\color{white}\textbf{Question 1} \hfill \small True / False \textbar{} Topic: Network Security Concepts}{tfcol}
Network security in the narrow sense only refers to national cybersecurity laws.

\begin{qoptions}
\item True
\item False
\end{qoptions}
\begin{explainbox}{False}
\textbf{Concept:} Narrow-sense network security covers vendor solutions and enterprise network protection, not only national laws.
\end{explainbox}
\end{qcard}

\begin{qcard}{\color{white}\textbf{Question 2} \hfill \small True / False \textbar{} Topic: Network Security Concepts}{tfcol}
The CIA triad includes confidentiality, integrity, and availability.

\begin{qoptions}
\item True
\item False
\end{qoptions}
\begin{explainbox}{True}
\textbf{Concept:} Confidentiality, integrity, and availability are the three foundational goals of information security.
\end{explainbox}
\end{qcard}

\begin{qcard}{\color{white}\textbf{Question 3} \hfill \small True / False \textbar{} Topic: Network Security Concepts}{tfcol}
Building network security helps enterprises reduce losses from attacks.

\begin{qoptions}
\item True
\item False
\end{qoptions}
\begin{explainbox}{True}
\textbf{Concept:} Security controls reduce the impact of breaches, downtime, and data loss, thereby lowering financial losses.
\end{explainbox}
\end{qcard}

\begin{qcard}{\color{white}\textbf{Question 4} \hfill \small True / False \textbar{} Topic: Network Security Concepts}{tfcol}
Non-repudiation ensures that senders or receivers cannot deny their actions.

\begin{qoptions}
\item True
\item False
\end{qoptions}
\begin{explainbox}{True}
\textbf{Concept:} Non-repudiation provides evidence to prevent denial of participation.
\end{explainbox}
\end{qcard}

\begin{qcard}{\color{white}\textbf{Question 5} \hfill \small True / False \textbar{} Topic: Network Security Concepts}{tfcol}
Availability means anyone can access the system at any time.

\begin{qoptions}
\item True
\item False
\end{qoptions}
\begin{explainbox}{False}
\textbf{Concept:} Availability means authorized users can access information when required, not unlimited access.
\end{explainbox}
\end{qcard}

\begin{qcard}{\color{white}\textbf{Question 6} \hfill \small True / False \textbar{} Topic: Network Security Concepts}{tfcol}
Zero trust means everything inside the network is trusted by default.

\begin{qoptions}
\item True
\item False
\end{qoptions}
\begin{explainbox}{False}
\textbf{Concept:} Zero trust never assumes trust based on network location and verifies every request.
\end{explainbox}
\end{qcard}

\begin{qcard}{\color{white}\textbf{Question 7} \hfill \small True / False \textbar{} Topic: Network Security Concepts}{tfcol}
Integrity ensures that data is not tampered with during transmission.

\begin{qoptions}
\item True
\item False
\end{qoptions}
\begin{explainbox}{True}
\textbf{Concept:} Integrity protects against unauthorized modification or destruction of data.
\end{explainbox}
\end{qcard}

\begin{qcard}{\color{white}\textbf{Question 8} \hfill \small True / False \textbar{} Topic: Network Security Concepts}{tfcol}
Network security broadly refers to cyberspace security at a national level.

\begin{qoptions}
\item True
\item False
\end{qoptions}
\begin{explainbox}{True}
\textbf{Concept:} Broad network security covers national cyberspace protection.
\end{explainbox}
\end{qcard}

\begin{qcard}{\color{white}\textbf{Question 9} \hfill \small True / False \textbar{} Topic: Network Security Concepts}{tfcol}
The CIA triad excludes availability.

\begin{qoptions}
\item True
\item False
\end{qoptions}
\begin{explainbox}{False}
\textbf{Concept:} The CIA triad includes confidentiality, integrity, and availability.
\end{explainbox}
\end{qcard}

Risk is the combination of threat likelihood and impact.

\item True
\item False
\end{qoptions}

\section{Network Fundamentals}
\begin{qcard}{\color{white}\textbf{Question 1} \hfill \small True / False \textbar{} Topic: Network Fundamentals}{tfcol}
The OSI model was developed before the TCP/IP model.

\begin{qoptions}
\item True
\item False
\end{qoptions}
\begin{explainbox}{False}
\textbf{Concept:} TCP/IP was developed earlier and became the de facto standard; OSI was later as a reference model.
\end{explainbox}
\end{qcard}

\begin{qcard}{\color{white}\textbf{Question 2} \hfill \small True / False \textbar{} Topic: Network Fundamentals}{tfcol}
DNS always uses TCP port 53.

\begin{qoptions}
\item True
\item False
\end{qoptions}
\begin{explainbox}{False}
\textbf{Concept:} DNS primarily uses UDP port 53; TCP port 53 is used for zone transfers and large responses.
\end{explainbox}
\end{qcard}

\begin{qcard}{\color{white}\textbf{Question 3} \hfill \small True / False \textbar{} Topic: Network Fundamentals}{tfcol}
A switch forwards packets based on IP addresses.

\begin{qoptions}
\item True
\item False
\end{qoptions}
\begin{explainbox}{False}
\textbf{Concept:} A switch forwards frames based on MAC addresses; routers forward packets based on IP addresses.
\end{explainbox}
\end{qcard}

\begin{qcard}{\color{white}\textbf{Question 4} \hfill \small True / False \textbar{} Topic: Network Fundamentals}{tfcol}
IPv4 addresses are 32 bits long.

\begin{qoptions}
\item True
\item False
\end{qoptions}
\begin{explainbox}{True}
\textbf{Concept:} IPv4 addresses are 32-bit binary numbers, usually written in dotted-decimal notation.
\end{explainbox}
\end{qcard}

\begin{qcard}{\color{white}\textbf{Question 5} \hfill \small True / False \textbar{} Topic: Network Fundamentals}{tfcol}
ICMP is a transport-layer protocol.

\begin{qoptions}
\item True
\item False
\end{qoptions}
\begin{explainbox}{False}
\textbf{Concept:} ICMP is a network-layer protocol used for diagnostics and error reporting.
\end{explainbox}
\end{qcard}

\begin{qcard}{\color{white}\textbf{Question 6} \hfill \small True / False \textbar{} Topic: Network Fundamentals}{tfcol}
TCP provides connection-oriented reliable delivery.

\begin{qoptions}
\item True
\item False
\end{qoptions}
\begin{explainbox}{True}
\textbf{Concept:} TCP establishes connections and ensures reliable, ordered delivery.
\end{explainbox}
\end{qcard}

\begin{qcard}{\color{white}\textbf{Question 7} \hfill \small True / False \textbar{} Topic: Network Fundamentals}{tfcol}
A hub creates separate collision domains per port.

\begin{qoptions}
\item True
\item False
\end{qoptions}
\begin{explainbox}{False}
\textbf{Concept:} A hub shares a single collision domain; switches create separate collision domains.
\end{explainbox}
\end{qcard}

IPv6 addresses are 128 bits long.

\begin{qoptions}
\item True
\item False
\end{qoptions}
\begin{explainbox}{True}
\textbf{Concept:} IPv6 addresses are 128-bit binary numbers.
\end{explainbox}
\end{qcard}

\section{OSI and TCP/IP Models}
\begin{qcard}{\color{white}\textbf{Question 1} \hfill \small True / False \textbar{} Topic: OSI and TCP/IP Models}{tfcol}
The TCP/IP model combines the OSI session and presentation layers into the application layer.

\begin{qoptions}
\item True
\item False
\end{qoptions}
\begin{explainbox}{True}
\textbf{Concept:} The TCP/IP model does not have separate session and presentation layers; their functions are included in the application layer.
\end{explainbox}
\end{qcard}

\section{Network Protocols}
\begin{qcard}{\color{white}\textbf{Question 1} \hfill \small True / False \textbar{} Topic: Network Protocols}{tfcol}
UDP guarantees ordered delivery of packets.

\begin{qoptions}
\item True
\item False
\end{qoptions}
\begin{explainbox}{False}
\textbf{Concept:} UDP is connectionless and does not guarantee delivery, ordering, or duplicate protection.
\end{explainbox}
\end{qcard}

\section{Network Devices}
\begin{qcard}{\color{white}\textbf{Question 1} \hfill \small True / False \textbar{} Topic: Network Devices}{tfcol}
A router forwards frames based on MAC addresses.

\begin{qoptions}
\item True
\item False
\end{qoptions}
\begin{explainbox}{False}
\textbf{Concept:} Routers forward packets based on IP addresses; switches forward frames based on MAC addresses.
\end{explainbox}
\end{qcard}

\begin{qcard}{\color{white}\textbf{Question 2} \hfill \small True / False \textbar{} Topic: Network Devices}{tfcol}
A switch creates a separate collision domain for each port.

\begin{qoptions}
\item True
\item False
\end{qoptions}
\begin{explainbox}{True}
\textbf{Concept:} Each switch port is its own collision domain, reducing collisions compared to a hub.
\end{explainbox}
\end{qcard}

\section{Enterprise Security Threats}
\begin{qcard}{\color{white}\textbf{Question 1} \hfill \small True / False \textbar{} Topic: Enterprise Security Threats}{tfcol}
A worm requires a host program to spread.

\begin{qoptions}
\item True
\item False
\end{qoptions}
\begin{explainbox}{False}
\textbf{Concept:} Worms are standalone malware that can self-replicate; viruses require host programs.
\end{explainbox}
\end{qcard}

\begin{qcard}{\color{white}\textbf{Question 2} \hfill \small True / False \textbar{} Topic: Enterprise Security Threats}{tfcol}
Phishing attacks target human psychology rather than software vulnerabilities.

\begin{qoptions}
\item True
\item False
\end{qoptions}
\begin{explainbox}{True}
\textbf{Concept:} Phishing uses deception and social engineering to trick users.
\end{explainbox}
\end{qcard}

\begin{qcard}{\color{white}\textbf{Question 3} \hfill \small True / False \textbar{} Topic: Enterprise Security Threats}{tfcol}
Using HTTP instead of HTTPS improves confidentiality.

\begin{qoptions}
\item True
\item False
\end{qoptions}
\begin{explainbox}{False}
\textbf{Concept:} HTTP sends data in plaintext; HTTPS encrypts data to protect confidentiality.
\end{explainbox}
\end{qcard}

\begin{qcard}{\color{white}\textbf{Question 4} \hfill \small True / False \textbar{} Topic: Enterprise Security Threats}{tfcol}
The DMZ zone is typically more trusted than the Untrust zone but less trusted than the Trust zone.

\begin{qoptions}
\item True
\item False
\end{qoptions}
\begin{explainbox}{True}
\textbf{Concept:} DMZ has an intermediate trust level for public-facing servers.
\end{explainbox}
\end{qcard}

\begin{qcard}{\color{white}\textbf{Question 5} \hfill \small True / False \textbar{} Topic: Enterprise Security Threats}{tfcol}
Patch management helps eliminate known vulnerabilities on hosts.

\begin{qoptions}
\item True
\item False
\end{qoptions}
\begin{explainbox}{True}
\textbf{Concept:} Applying patches closes security holes that attackers could exploit.
\end{explainbox}
\end{qcard}

\begin{qcard}{\color{white}\textbf{Question 6} \hfill \small True / False \textbar{} Topic: Enterprise Security Threats}{tfcol}
All security threats originate from external attackers.

\begin{qoptions}
\item True
\item False
\end{qoptions}
\begin{explainbox}{False}
\textbf{Concept:} Threats can be external or internal, accidental or malicious.

\begin{qcard}{\color{white}\textbf{Question 1} \hfill \small True / False \textbar{} Topic: Firewall Basics}{tfcol}

\begin{qcard}{\color{white}\textbf{Question 2} \hfill \small True / False \textbar{} Topic: Firewall Basics}{tfcol}

\begin{qoptions}
\item True
\item False
\end{qoptions}

\end{explainbox}
\end{qcard}

\section{GRE VPN}
\begin{qcard}{\color{white}\textbf{Question 1} \hfill \small True / False \textbar{} Topic: GRE VPN}{tfcol}
GRE provides strong encryption by default.

\begin{qoptions}
\item True
\item False
\end{qoptions}
\begin{explainbox}{False}
\textbf{Concept:} GRE only encapsulates traffic; encryption must be added separately, often with IPsec.
\end{explainbox}
\end{qcard}

\section{IPsec VPN}
\begin{qcard}{\color{white}\textbf{Question 1} \hfill \small True / False \textbar{} Topic: IPsec VPN}{tfcol}
ESP provides both encryption and authentication.

\begin{qoptions}
\item True
\item False
\end{qoptions}
\begin{explainbox}{True}
\textbf{Concept:} ESP can encrypt payloads and authenticate packets.
\end{explainbox}
\end{qcard}

\begin{qcard}{\color{white}\textbf{Question 2} \hfill \small True / False \textbar{} Topic: IPsec VPN}{tfcol}
AH provides payload encryption.

\begin{qoptions}
\item True
\item False
\end{qoptions}
\begin{explainbox}{False}
\textbf{Concept:} AH provides authentication and integrity but does not encrypt the payload.
\end{explainbox}
\end{qcard}

\section{L2TP VPN}
\begin{qcard}{\color{white}\textbf{Question 1} \hfill \small True / False \textbar{} Topic: L2TP VPN}{tfcol}
L2TP alone does not provide encryption.

\begin{qoptions}
\item True
\item False
\end{qoptions}
\begin{explainbox}{True}

\end{explainbox}
\end{qcard}

\section{SSL VPN}
\begin{qcard}{\color{white}\textbf{Question 1} \hfill \small True / False \textbar{} Topic: SSL VPN}{tfcol}
